I mean, I get distracted and lose my place, but I haven’t really had times where I couldn’t tell what was real or felt like my thoughts were jumbled like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like hearing voices that others don’t hear, or seeing things that others don’t see, or having beliefs that others don’t share?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had anything like that. I don’t hear voices or see things other people don’t, and I don’t think I’ve had beliefs like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted easily in other parts of your life, like at home or while driving, or is this mostly happening at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I really notice it. The warehouse has all those beepers and forklift alarms, or someone moving a pallet nearby, and I get pulled off what I’m counting. Then I look back and I’ve put the wrong numbers into the inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or in quieter places, do you still find yourself getting distracted easily, or does it seem to only happen in the busy environment of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: At home it’s not as bad. I might lose my place sometimes, but what’s really been causing problems is the warehouse. A beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my attention just jumps to that. Then I go back to the count and realize I’ve entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially around the same time these attention issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed feeling especially hopeless or losing interest in things around the same time. It’s more that the attention problem stands out to me at work.\n\nDoctor: When you're in quieter places, like at home or in a calm environment, do you find yourself losing focus or getting distracted often, even when there aren’t beepers or forklift alarms around?\n\nPatient: Not really often, no. In quieter places I can usually do okay. It’s mainly at the warehouse—if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, or somebody moves a pallet near me, I lose the count. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. If I’m reading or talking one-on-one, I can usually stay with it. The problem is more at the warehouse—any beeper or forklift alarm, or someone moving a pallet, pulls my mind off what I’m counting. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

If I’m reading or talking one-on-one, I can usually stay with it. The problem is more at the warehouse—any beeper or forklift alarm, or someone moving a pallet, pulls my mind off what I’m counting. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that requires focus, like filling out paperwork or following instructions step by step, do you find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes, even if it's quiet around you?\n\nPatient: If it’s quiet, I don’t notice it as much. I’ll be counting or putting inventory numbers in, and then a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or somebody moves a pallet, and it pulls me right off what I was doing. Then I look back and I’ve put the wrong numbers into the inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or in quieter places, do you still find yourself getting distracted easily, or does it seem to only happen in the busy environment of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: At home it’s not as bad. I might lose my place sometimes, but what’s really been causing problems is the warehouse. A beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my attention just jumps to that. Then I go back to the count and realize I’ve entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially around the same time these attention issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed feeling especially hopeless or losing interest in things around the same time. It’s more that the attention problem stands out to me at work.\n\nDoctor: When you're in quieter places, like at home or in a calm environment, do you find yourself losing focus or getting distracted often, even when there aren’t beepers or forklift alarms around?\n\nPatient: Not really often, no. In quieter places I can usually do okay. It’s mainly at the warehouse—if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, or somebody moves a pallet near me, I lose the count. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. If I’m reading or talking one-on-one, I can usually stay with it. The problem is more at the warehouse—any beeper or forklift alarm, or someone moving a pallet, pulls my mind off what I’m counting. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that requires focus, like filling out paperwork or following instructions step by step, do you find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes, even if it's quiet around you?\n\nPatient: If it’s quiet, I don’t notice it as much. The real problem is at the warehouse. I’ll be counting or putting inventory numbers in, and then a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or somebody moves a pallet, and it pulls me right off what I was doing. Then I go back and realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a stressful situation, like being in a hurry or under pressure, even in quiet places, do you find yourself getting distracted more easily or making mistakes because your mind is pulled away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when I’m under pressure it can be worse, but the main place I notice it is still at work. Then I go back and later realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you still find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes if you're in a rush or feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: At home, if it’s quiet, I don’t really notice it as much, even if I’m trying to get something done. Then later I see I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you ever find your mind wandering or making mistakes because you can't concentrate, even if you're not being interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I notice that much at home. If it’s quiet and nobody’s interrupting me, I usually don’t think of it as a big problem. I mean, I might make a mistake here or there like anybody, but it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something at home that requires focus, like reading or organizing something, do you ever find yourself losing track of what you're doing without any outside distractions pulling your attention away?\n\nPatient: Not really at home, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more when I’m at the warehouse. I’ll be in the middle of a count and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet, and I lose where I was. Then I find out later I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home doing something that doesn't require much focus, like watching TV or sitting quietly, do you ever catch yourself losing track of what's happening or getting distracted by things around you, even when you're not stressed or in a hurry?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m just sitting at home watching TV. Then I lose my place and later realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. If I’m reading or talking one-on-one, I can usually stay with it. The problem is more at the warehouse—any beeper or forklift alarm, or someone moving a pallet, pulls my mind off what I’m counting. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that requires focus, like filling out paperwork or following instructions step by step, do you find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes, even if it's quiet around you?\n\nPatient: If it’s quiet, I don’t notice it as much. The real problem is at the warehouse. I’ll be counting or putting inventory numbers in, and then a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or somebody moves a pallet, and it pulls me right off what I was doing. Then I go back and realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a stressful situation, like being in a hurry or under pressure, even in quiet places, do you find yourself getting distracted more easily or making mistakes because your mind is pulled away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when I’m under pressure it can be worse, but the main place I notice it is still at work. If I’m trying to hurry through a count and then a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, I lose where I was. Then I go back and later realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you still find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes if you're in a rush or feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: At home, if it’s quiet, I don’t really notice it as much, even if I’m trying to get something done. Then later I see I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you ever find your mind wandering or making mistakes because you can't concentrate, even if you're not being interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I notice that much at home. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that requires focus, like filling out paperwork or following instructions step by step, do you find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes, even if it's quiet around you?\n\nPatient: If it’s quiet, I don’t notice it as much. Then later I see I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you ever find your mind wandering or making mistakes because you can't concentrate, even if you're not being interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I notice that much at home. I might lose my place sometimes, but what’s really been causing problems is the warehouse. A beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my attention just jumps to that. Then I go back to the count and realize I’ve entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially around the same time these attention issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. Like if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, or I see someone moving a pallet, I just lose my place in the count. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in the inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your thoughts seemed jumbled or when you had trouble telling if something was real or just in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get distracted and lose my place, but I haven’t really had times where I couldn’t tell what was real or felt like my thoughts were jumbled like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like hearing voices that others don’t hear, or seeing things that others don’t see, or having beliefs that others don’t share?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had anything like that. I don’t hear voices or see things other people don’t, and I don’t think I’ve had beliefs like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted easily in other parts of your life, like at home or while driving, or is this mostly happening at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I really notice it. The warehouse has all those beepers and forklift alarms, or someone moving a pallet nearby, and I get pulled off what I’m counting. Then I look back and I’ve put the wrong numbers into the inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or in quieter places, do you still find yourself getting distracted easily, or does it seem to only happen in the busy environment of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: At home it’s not as bad. I might lose my place sometimes, but what’s really been causing problems is the warehouse. A beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my attention just jumps to that. Then I go back to the count and realize I’ve entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially around the same time these attention issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed feeling especially hopeless or losing interest in things around the same time. It’s more that the attention problem stands out to me at work.\n\nDoctor: When you're in quieter places, like at home or in a calm environment, do you find yourself losing focus or getting distracted often, even when there aren’t beepers or forklift alarms around?\n\nPatient: Not really often, no. In quieter places I can usually do okay. It’s mainly at the warehouse—if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, or somebody moves a pallet near me, I lose the count. Then when I go back to it, I’ll realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a quiet place, like reading a book or talking with someone one-on-one, do you find it hard to stay focused or do you catch yourself zoning out?\n\nPatient: In a quiet place, not so much. If I’m reading or talking one-on-one, I can usually stay with it. The problem is more at the warehouse—any beeper or forklift alarm, or someone moving a pallet, pulls my mind off what I’m counting. Then I go back and realize I put the wrong numbers in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that requires focus, like filling out paperwork or following instructions step by step, do you find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes, even if it's quiet around you?\n\nPatient: If it’s quiet, I don’t notice it as much. The real problem is at the warehouse. I’ll be counting or putting inventory numbers in, and then a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or somebody moves a pallet, and it pulls me right off what I was doing. Then I go back and realize I entered the wrong numbers.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a stressful situation, like being in a hurry or under pressure, even in quiet places, do you find yourself getting distracted more easily or making mistakes because your mind is pulled away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when I’m under pressure it can be worse, but the main place I notice it is still at work. If I’m trying to hurry through a count and then a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, I lose where I was. Then I go back and later realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you still find yourself getting distracted or making mistakes if you're in a rush or feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: At home, if it’s quiet, I don’t really notice it as much, even if I’m trying to get something done. What’s bringing me in is more that at the warehouse I can’t keep my mind on one thing—like I’ll be doing a count and a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or somebody moves a pallet, and I lose my place. Then later I see I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that needs focus, like filling out forms or following steps carefully, but you're not in a noisy place—say, at home—do you ever find your mind wandering or making mistakes because you can't concentrate, even if you're not being interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I notice that much at home. If it’s quiet and nobody’s interrupting me, I usually don’t think of it as a big problem. I mean, I might make a mistake here or there like anybody, but it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something at home that requires focus, like reading or organizing something, do you ever find yourself losing track of what you're doing without any outside distractions pulling your attention away?\n\nPatient: Not really at home, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more when I’m at the warehouse. I’ll be in the middle of a count and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet, and I lose where I was. Then I find out later I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home doing something that doesn't require much focus, like watching TV or sitting quietly, do you ever catch yourself losing track of what's happening or getting distracted by things around you, even when you're not stressed or in a hurry?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m just sitting at home watching TV. What I keep noticing is at work—at the warehouse, if I’m doing a count and a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet, it pulls me right off. Then I lose my place and later realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not stressed or in a hurry, like when you're doing something simple and quiet at home, do you ever catch yourself losing focus or having trouble staying on task?\n\nPatient: At home when it’s quiet, I don’t really notice it as much. Then I lose my place, and later I’ll realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a very quiet place, like sitting alone in a room or listening to a calm conversation, do you ever find it hard to stay focused or notice your mind drifting off, even if there's nothing pulling your attention away?\n\nPatient: In a really quiet room, I don’t notice it as much. What I’m trying to explain is more that I can’t keep my mind on one thing at work anymore. Like if I’m doing a count at the warehouse and a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet, I get pulled off it, and then later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a very quiet place, like sitting alone in a room or listening to a calm conversation, do you ever find it hard to stay focused or notice your mind drifting off even if there's nothing pulling your attention away?\n\nPatient: Honestly, in a quiet room I don’t notice it as much.
